## Codex · 2026-06-15T20:55:36.909Z · final_answer

Next in the plan is **prove the core loop works end-to-end with Naomi’s real data**, then turn on personalization.

I’d do it in this order:

1. **Live voice-email QA**
   Run the app locally, sign in, start a real inbox session, and verify:
   `reply → draft → send`, `archive`, `skip`, `wrap`, follow-up memory, and queue advancement all behave correctly.

2. **Production readiness**
   Check Supabase migrations, especially `002_multi_account.sql`, verify env vars, cron secrets, Gmail/Calendar OAuth, then deploy/redeploy the current apps.

3. **LoRA data audit**
   Hit `/api/lora/export` and inspect Naomi’s generated examples. Make sure the training pairs actually look like:
   received email/context → Naomi-style reply.
   Then download JSONL with `/api/lora/export?format=jsonl`.

4. **Trigger first personal model training**
   Configure RunPod, run `/api/lora/train`, confirm adapter status/webhook/deployment, then evaluate whether drafts improve.

5. **Wire personal LoRA into voice-email drafts**
   Right now voice-email drafts use profile + past emails + Haiku. The next Cortex-level leap is:
   deployed personal adapter first, Claude/Haiku fallback second.

6. **Build “Plan My Day”**
   This is the next true Cortex agent from the recovered plans: combine Gmail, Calendar, open loops, memory, and profile into one daily voice/chat flow:
   “What do I have to do today?”
   It should produce a calm prioritized day plan, not a dashboard dump.

7. **Add message/social ingestion**
   Start with the practical sources from the research:
   iMessage local `chat.db`, GroupMe API, exported WhatsApp/Instagram data later.

So the immediate next work package is: **end-to-end QA + deploy + LoRA export/training audit**. After that, the next product feature is **Plan My Day**.

## Codex · 2026-06-19T15:23:41.057Z · final_answer

Yes. The shortest true version:

LoRA setup has 4 parts:

1. **Training data**
   Your sent emails become examples of “how Naomi writes.”

2. **Trainer**
   RunPod trains a small adapter file from those examples.

3. **Storage**
   The adapter gets saved to S3/R2/Supabase Storage.

4. **Inference server**
   LoRAX loads the base model plus your adapter and answers in your style.

For Cortex specifically, most of the app code is already wired. What remains is infrastructure.

**Step 1: Restore Supabase**
Your current blocker is still Supabase DNS:

```text
addkjndgmbxiulpquizn.supabase.co
```

That project must be restored or replaced. Then apply all migrations, including:

```text
001_initial_schema.sql
...
007_developer_api_keys.sql
```

Then run:

```bash
cd /Users/naomiivie/cortex
npm run check:migrations
npm run verify:schema
```

**Step 2: Get enough email data**
LoRA should train on your sent emails. Minimum in code is `100`, but realistically:

```text
100 = test run
250 = usable
500+ = actually good
```

After Gmail sync, check:

```bash
curl https://cortex-web-one.vercel.app/api/lora/status
curl https://cortex-web-one.vercel.app/api/lora/eval
```

`/api/lora/eval` tells you if the data is safe to train on.

**Step 3: Set up adapter storage**
Use S3 or an S3-compatible bucket like Cloudflare R2.

Add these to `cortex-web` Vercel envs:

```bash
S3_BUCKET=
S3_REGION=
S3_ACCESS_KEY=
[REDACTED:s3-access-key]
S3_ENDPOINT= # optional for R2
```

Cortex expects adapters at paths like:

```text
users/<user-id>/lora-adapters/v1/adapter.tar.gz
```

**Step 4: Set up RunPod training**
Use the worker already scaffolded here:

```text
/Users/naomiivie/cortex/runpod-lora-worker/
```

Deploy that worker to RunPod Serverless, then add:

```bash
RUNPOD_API_KEY=
[REDACTED:runpod-api-key]
LORA_WEBHOOK_SECRET=
```

When you click “Train Personal Model” in Settings, Cortex sends the training job to RunPod.

**Step 5: Set up LoRAX inference**
LoRAX is the server that uses:

```text
base model + Naomi adapter
```

Add to both `cortex-web` and `voice-email`:

```bash
LORAX_API_URL=
LORAX_API_KEY=
```

Then Cortex can route simple/medium requests through your personal adapter before falling back to Claude.

**Mental Model**
LoRA does not store your life facts. That is RAG/memory.

LoRA learns:

```text
your tone
your sentence rhythm
your email style
your “how I would say this”
```

Cortex uses:

```text
RAG = what Naomi knows / context
LoRA = how Naomi sounds
Claude = reasoning fallback
```

So the real setup order is:

```text
Supabase works
→ Gmail data synced
→ eval passes
→ S3 bucket exists
→ RunPod worker deployed
→ LoRAX server deployed
→ Vercel envs added
→ click Train Personal Model
```

## Codex · 2026-06-19T15:23:50.804Z · commentary

## Codex · 2026-06-19T15:24:19.298Z · final_answer

Yeah, you read it right. Supabase currently gives **two active free projects** total across orgs where you’re Owner/Admin; **paused projects don’t count**. So your options are:

1. **Free path:** pause or delete one old Supabase project, then create a new free Cortex project.
2. **Clean path:** pay for Supabase Pro for a Cortex org.
3. **Hacky path:** make a third account. It works sometimes, but I would not build Cortex production on a workaround like that.

My recommendation: **if one of your two free projects is disposable, pause it and use the free slot for Cortex first.** If both existing projects matter, pay for Supabase Pro for Cortex.

Why Pro may actually be worth it for Cortex:
- Cortex stores lots of email/calendar/message chunks plus embeddings.
- Supabase Free has a **500 MB database size limit** and can go read-only after that.
- Pro gives more breathing room, backups, and no project pausing.
- Supabase billing is org-based, so you can keep your two free projects in a separate free org while Cortex lives in a paid org. Supabase documents this directly. Sources: [billing docs](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/platform/billing-on-supabase), [billing FAQ](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/platform/billing-faq), [keeping free projects after Pro](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/troubleshooting/keeping-free-projects-after-pro-upgrade-Kf9Xm2).

For Cortex, I’d do this:

```text
Create new Supabase project
→ copy its URL, anon key, service role key
→ update cortex-web + voice-email envs
→ run migrations 001 through 007
→ run npm run verify:schema
→ redeploy both apps
```

If you want cheapest-first: pause an old free project and use free Supabase until Cortex’s data gets too big. If you want “stop fighting infra and build the actual product”: pay for Pro.

## Codex · 2026-06-27T20:47:13.628Z · final_answer

Yes. The feature we most rushed over was **the actual per-user LoRA becoming real**, not just scaffolded.

I wrote the March-only review here: [MARCH_ORIGINAL_LORA_PLAN_GAP_REVIEW.md](/Users/naomiivie/cortex/research/MARCH_ORIGINAL_LORA_PLAN_GAP_REVIEW.md)

Original plan, in the per-user LoRA version:

1. Ingest the user’s whole digital life: Gmail, Calendar, Instagram, GroupMe, WhatsApp, iMessage, Notes/Notion, later bank data.
2. Build deep profile + memory + RAG.
3. Train a **per-user QLoRA adapter** on their actual data.
4. Serve that adapter through LoRAX/vLLM.
5. Use LoRA for voice/style/personal patterns, RAG for fresh context, Claude for complex reasoning.
6. Re-train monthly.
7. Later move toward on-device MLX/Core ML and hybrid personal-model + cloud-model routing.

The main rushed/softened pieces:

- **LoRA quality bar got lowered**: March research said 500 examples minimum, 1,000 viable, 2,000-5,000 recommended. Current code allows training at 100.
- **Email importance was hand-waved** into “profile + LoRA will learn it,” but LoRA never actually trained.
- **Multi-source ingestion is incomplete**: Gmail/Calendar exist; Instagram, GroupMe, WhatsApp, Plaid are still “coming soon,” with import workarounds.
- **Auto-spheres are thinner than intended** because they need whole-life data, not just email/calendar.
- **Top-layer actions are narrow**: email/calendar mostly, not DMs, groceries, Apple Pay, posting.
- **Voice was core originally**, but it drifted into a separate voice-email path instead of being fully inside Cortex.
- **Dreams/notes/forgotten-things resurfacing** was in the original soul of it, but not central in the web build.
- **Developer platform** exists as API-key/query skeleton, not the full “Stripe for personal AI” engine.

Short version: we built a lot of the skeleton, but the March vision was **Cortex as a personal model system**. The thing to return to is: get enough training data, enforce the original LoRA data threshold, run the first real adapter, and make the app’s core answers/drafts visibly pass through the personal model.

Yes. Following the original plan, the next move is:

   - Run the formatter.
   - Inspect examples manually.
   - Remove junk: quoted threads, auto-replies, signatures, weird scraped content.
   - Goal: clean email-reply pairs.

4. **Run one real LoRA training test**
   - Use RunPod/Unsloth/QLoRA.
   - Base model: probably Qwen3-4B or Llama 3.2 3B, per original plan.
   - Train one adapter for you.

5. **Store and serve the adapter**
   - Store adapter in Supabase Storage or S3.
   - Connect LoRAX/vLLM.
   - Make `/api/chat` and email drafts actually use the adapter when deployed.

6. **Evaluate the difference**
   - Ask:
     - “Draft this email in my tone.”
     - “What do I have to do today?”
     - “What emails matter?”
   - Compare Claude-only vs RAG+Claude vs LoRA+RAG+Claude.

7. **Only after that: return to integrations**
   - Instagram
   - GroupMe
   - WhatsApp/iMessage import
   - Notes
   - Dream/forgotten-things resurfacing
   - Voice inside Cortex

So the immediate next task is:

**Step 1: inspect the current Gmail/sent-email training data and LoRA pipeline, then fix the threshold + export/eval path.**

That gets us back onto the original March plan instead of drifting into more UI.

## Codex · 2026-06-29T19:45:36.110Z · final_answer

Yes, it is possible. The sane version is not a loophole, it is a permissioned native Mac app.

I wrote the full research memo here: [CORTEX_MAC_FLOATING_ASSISTANT_DEEP_RESEARCH.md](/Users/naomiivie/cortex/research/CORTEX_MAC_FLOATING_ASSISTANT_DEEP_RESEARCH.md)

**Core answer**
Cortex can float on your Mac as:
- a menu bar app plus global hotkey/floating panel
- a background login agent
- a permission broker for Screen Recording, Accessibility, Full Disk Access or chosen folders, Calendar/Reminders, Gmail, browser context, etc.
- a local memory/index
- an action layer with approval before sending/deleting/posting

Apple explicitly lets users decide which apps can record screen/system audio, and macOS privacy settings include Full Disk Access, Accessibility, Automation, microphone, and screen recording controls. So the path is “ask clearly and get permission,” not “bypass macOS.” ([support.apple.com](https://support.apple.com/guide/mac-help/control-access-screen-system-audio-recording-mchld6aa7d23/mac)) ([support.apple.com](https://support.apple.com/guide/mac-help/change-privacy-security-settings-on-mac-mchl211c911f/mac))

**Claude comparison**
Claude does not magically have permanent Mac omniscience. Claude computer-use works through tools that your app/environment runs, and Anthropic specifically says to inform users and get consent before enabling those permissions. ([platform.claude.com](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/tool-use/computer-use-tool))

**Tailscale**
Tailscale is useful because it makes your Mac reachable privately without exposing it to the public internet. It uses WireGuard encrypted tunnels, coordination servers for key/address discovery, and DERP relays when direct connections fail. Serve can expose a local Mac service only inside your tailnet. ([tailscale.com](https://tailscale.com/blog/how-tailscale-works)) ([tailscale.com](https://tailscale.com/blog/how-tailscale-works)) ([tailscale.com](https://tailscale.com/blog/how-tailscale-works)) ([tailscale.com](https://tailscale.com/docs/features/tailscale-serve))

**Most important recommendation**
Do not start with “see everything forever.” Start with magical on-demand Cortex:
1. Hotkey opens floating Cortex.
2. It sees the active screen only when asked.
3. It uses Gmail/calendar/Cortex memory.
4. It drafts/acts only with approval.
5. Then add ambient memory source-by-source.

Next build step: scaffold `cortex-mac`, a signed SwiftUI/AppKit menu bar app with a floating panel, global hotkey, login item, and a permissions screen.

## Codex · 2026-06-29T19:59:05.964Z · final_answer

We’re far from the **always-on Mac assistant**, but not starting from zero.

Current Cortex is about **60% of the cloud brain/backend**, and only about **5-10% of the always-on Mac layer**.

What we already have:
- Web app: dashboard, chat, settings, onboarding.
- Auth/users via Clerk + Supabase.
- Gmail OAuth now works for your current account.
- Gmail/calendar ingestion infrastructure.
- Memory/profile/RAG pieces.
- Email agent UI and draft/send routes.
- Actions/audit trail scaffolding.
- LoRA training pipeline scaffolding and RunPod worker pieces.
- Cron/background jobs for cloud-side sync/briefing/profile maintenance.

What we do **not** have yet:
- No native Mac app.
- No menu bar/floating assistant.
- No login item/background daemon.
- No ScreenCaptureKit screen awareness.
- No Accessibility permission/UI tree reading.
- No Full Disk Access/user-folder watcher.
- No local Mac event timeline.
- No local vector memory.
- No Tailscale/private bridge.
- No proactive always-on loop watching what you’re doing.

So the honest shape is:

**Cortex Web = partially built brain.**
**Cortex Mac = not built yet.**
**Always-on personal assistant = requires new native layer.**

The good news is the web/backend work is still useful. The Mac app should plug into it, not replace it.

Next actual build step should be:

1. Create `cortex-mac/`
2. Build a SwiftUI/AppKit menu bar app
3. Add floating panel + global hotkey
4. Add launch-at-login
5. Add local agent process
6. Add permission screen for Accessibility + Screen Recording
7. Start logging active app/window locally

That gets us from “Cortex website” to “Cortex is alive on my Mac.”

## Naomi · 2026-06-29T20:01:26.475Z

## Codex · 2026-06-29T20:01:38.236Z · final_answer

Short answer: **the web app cannot truly see everything on your Mac by itself.** Browsers are sandboxed on purpose. A website can access things like camera/mic/screen share only after you choose them, and usually only while the tab/session is active. It cannot quietly watch your apps, read your files, inspect other windows, start at login, or control macOS like a real assistant.

So for the always-on Mac vision, **yes, it needs an app or local agent** eventually.

But we can absolutely do:

**Phase 1: Web First**
Ship Cortex Web as the brain:
- Gmail + calendar sync
- email agent
- memory/profile
- chat
- spheres/day planner
- LoRA training
- cloud jobs
- mobile-accessible dashboard

This is already the current path and is much closer.

**Phase 2: Mac Companion**
Add a small Mac app later that gives the web brain Mac senses:
- runs at login
- sees screen/window/app context with permission
- watches chosen folders
- floats over apps
- sends local context to Cortex Web
- receives tasks from Cortex Web

The best architecture is:

```text
Cortex Web = brain, account, memory, email, cloud sync
Cortex Mac = eyes/hands on your computer
Supabase = shared memory/action log
```

There is also a middle option: use the web app as a **PWA** or Electron/Tauri wrapper. But a plain PWA still cannot get deep Mac permissions. Electron/Tauri can, because then it is a desktop app wrapping web UI with native APIs.

My recommendation: **release web first**, but design it as if the Mac agent is coming. Add a `devices` / `local_context_events` layer soon, so when we build the Mac app it plugs in naturally.

## Codex · 2026-06-29T20:20:24.447Z · commentary

That expectation is emotionally correct, but OAuth is built differently: the access token is always short-lived, and “staying logged in” depends on a refresh token surviving. I’m going to check Google’s current rules and also test our active Gmail row without exposing tokens, because the reason can be policy or our implementation.

## Naomi · 2026-06-29T20:21:04.118Z

do we need a higher tier of supabase

## Codex · 2026-06-29T20:22:07.658Z · final_answer

No, not for the current blocker.

Supabase tier is **not** why Gmail token goes stale. Gmail uses short-lived access tokens; the important thing is whether the refresh token still works. I tested it:

- `nivie@wesleyan.edu`: refresh token works.
- `naomi.ivie04@gmail.com`: refresh token is dead/revoked.

So the immediate issue is **account/data cleanup + Gmail sync**, not Supabase capacity.

When we may need paid Supabase:
- If you already have 2 free active projects and need a third.
- Before real production launch.
- If we need reliable backups, more database/storage/egress, higher auth limits, no project pausing.
- When Mac assistant starts syncing lots of local event/memory data.

Supabase Free is enough for current dev/testing. Supabase Pro is likely worth it once Cortex becomes your real always-on brain, mostly for reliability/backups and not hitting free-project limits. Current Pro pricing is listed by Supabase as starting at `$25/month` per organization. Source: https://supabase.com/pricing

Open the Cortex project. It is the project whose URL matches your `cortex-web/.env.local` `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL`.

**Step 2: Open SQL Editor**
In the left sidebar, click `SQL Editor`.

Supabase’s SQL Editor is the dashboard place where you can run SQL against your database. Supabase docs also recommend proper migrations for version-controlled schema changes; we already created the migration file in the repo, but because this project is not CLI-linked from here, you’ll run the same SQL in the dashboard. Sources: https://supabase.com/features/sql-editor and https://supabase.com/docs/guides/deployment/database-migrations

Name it something like:

**Step 5: Click Run**
Click `Run`.

**Step 6: Verify**
Back in terminal, run:

```bash
cd /Users/naomiivie/cortex/cortex-web
npm run check:multi-account-schema
```

Expected result:

```text
Multi-account schema columns are ready.
```

- integrations UI
- Gmail OAuth callback
- Gmail sync per account
- email sending per account
- action approval token refresh
- schema checker

The one thing left before testing multiple Gmail accounts is: **run the SQL migration in Supabase.**

Hard answer: current Cortex is a strong **web/cloud brain**, but the “always-on AI assistant friend” requires two new layers:

2. **Native Mac app**: menu bar/floating assistant, launch-at-login agent, screen/UI awareness, local files, notifications, local timeline.

6. Demo: “Cortex found what I owe people and planned my day.”

For the hackathon: prove the loop in web first. Native Mac always-on comes after.

I grounded the doc in the current codebase plus platform docs: Apple [ScreenCaptureKit](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/screencapturekit), [SMAppService](https://developer.apple.com/documentation/servicemanagement/smappservice), Google [Gmail push](https://developers.google.com/workspace/gmail/api/guides/push), Google [Calendar push](https://developers.google.com/workspace/calendar/api/guides/push), Supabase [pgvector](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/extensions/pgvector), Supabase [Realtime](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/realtime/postgres-changes), and Tailscale [how it works](https://tailscale.com/blog/how-tailscale-works).

## Naomi · 2026-06-29T23:10:12.230Z

explainto me what you just did? wha tis hackathon assistant loop

## Codex · 2026-06-29T23:10:20.733Z · final_answer

I built the first real “Cortex acts like a personal assistant” loop.

Before this, Cortex could sync data and make a day plan, but it was mostly passive: you opened pages, asked questions, and it responded.

The **hackathon assistant loop** is the demo version of the always-on assistant brain:

1. It scans recent Gmail, Calendar, notes, and open loops.
2. It extracts:
   - people who matter
   - things you owe people
   - things people owe you
   - reminders
   - possible reply drafts
3. It saves those into real assistant tables:
   - `cortex_events`
   - `cortex_people`
   - `cortex_commitments`
   - `cortex_reminders`
   - `cortex_daily_plans`
   - `cortex_agent_runs`
4. It updates your daily plan.
5. It can queue email drafts into the existing **Actions** page so you approve before Cortex sends anything.
6. I added a dashboard button: **Run Cortex Loop**.

So the hackathon story becomes:

“Cortex reads my digital life, figures out what I have to do, who I need to follow up with, plans my day, and queues actions for approval.”

I pushed and deployed the code. The remaining blocker is Supabase: those new tables don’t exist in the live database yet, so the button won’t work until `009_assistant_loop.sql` is run in Supabase.

## Naomi · 2026-06-30T00:21:08.867Z

## Codex · 2026-06-30T00:21:14.314Z · commentary

I’m going to check current official Anthropic pricing before I give numbers, because model pricing changes and I do not want to guess here.

## Codex · 2026-06-30T00:21:40.622Z · final_answer

Current Cortex default is **Claude Opus 4.8**.

Official Anthropic pricing:
- **Opus 4.8:** `$5 / 1M input tokens` + `$25 / 1M output tokens`
- **Sonnet fallback:** about `$3 / 1M input` + `$15 / 1M output`

Source: Anthropic says Opus 4.8 regular usage is `$5` input and `$25` output per million tokens. [Anthropic Opus 4.8](https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-opus-4-8)

Rough Cortex costs:

| Action | Estimated cost |
|---|---:|
| Small chat response | `$0.02–$0.05` |
| Normal chat with profile + memory + RAG | `$0.05–$0.15` |
| Email draft | `$0.03–$0.12` |
| Profile build | `$0.30–$1.50` |
| Generate spheres | `$0.30–$1.50` |
| Run Cortex Loop | `$0.20–$1.00` usually, maybe more with huge context |

The expensive part is **output**, not input. Opus output costs 5x input.

For hackathon/demo use: probably fine. You might spend a few dollars to tens of dollars if you’re clicking a lot.

For real users at scale: we’ll need routing later:
- Opus for chat/planning/important reasoning.
- Sonnet for routine extraction.
- Haiku or batch jobs for background cleanup.
- Prompt caching for profile/memory context.

**What You Need To Do**
In Vercel, add these env vars to the Cortex project:

```bash
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN=
SENTRY_DSN=
SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN=
[REDACTED:sentry-auth-token]
SENTRY_PROJECT=
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT=production
SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT=production

ELEVENLABS_API_KEY=
[REDACTED:elevenlabs-api-key]
GROQ_API_KEY=
```

For “like Sabi,” the important ones are `ELEVENLABS_VOICE_ID` for the spoken voice and `GROQ_API_KEY` for fast speech-to-text.

After deploy, check:

```bash
https://cortex.eduforequality.org/api/voice/health
```

It should say `ready` once ElevenLabs + Groq are configured.

The backend voice path exists now, but the main Cortex UI does not yet have the mic/speaker loop. Next build step is wiring Ask Cortex so it records audio, sends it to `/api/voice/stt`, sends the transcript into `/api/chat`, then speaks the response through `/api/voice/tts`.

I did not deploy because `cortex-web` already has concurrent dirty UI files from other agents, and I didn’t want to ship over their work blindly.

Sources checked: [Sentry Next.js manual setup](https://docs.sentry.io/platforms/javascript/guides/nextjs/manual-setup/), [Groq speech-to-text docs](https://console.groq.com/docs/speech-to-text), [ElevenLabs text-to-speech docs](https://elevenlabs.io/docs/api-reference/text-to-speech).

## Codex · 2026-06-30T05:36:42.254Z · commentary

I’ll give you the literal click-by-click path for the parts I just added: Sentry, ElevenLabs, Groq, Vercel env vars, deploy, and the final health check. I’m quickly verifying the current official docs/URLs so the steps don’t drift on you.

## Codex · 2026-06-30T05:37:33.504Z · final_answer

Yep. Do it in this order.

**1. Sentry**
Go to [sentry.io](https://sentry.io) and sign in.

1. Click `Create Project`.
2. Choose `Next.js`.
3. Name it `cortex-web`.
4. After it creates the project, find the `DSN`.
   - Usually: `Project Settings` → `Client Keys (DSN)`.
5. Copy that DSN.

You will use the same DSN for both:

```bash
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN=your_dsn_here
SENTRY_DSN=your_dsn_here
```

Then get these:

```bash
SENTRY_ORG=your_org_slug
SENTRY_PROJECT=your_project_slug
```

Usually they are in the Sentry URL. If your URL looks like:

```bash
https://naomi.sentry.io/projects/cortex-web/
```

then likely:

```bash
SENTRY_ORG=naomi
SENTRY_PROJECT=cortex-web
```

Then make an auth token:

1. In Sentry, go to `Settings`.
2. Find `Developer Settings` → `Auth Tokens` or `Organization Tokens`.
3. Create a token for source map upload.
4. Copy it once.

That becomes:

```bash
SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN=[REDACTED:sentry-auth-token]
```

**2. ElevenLabs**
Go to [ElevenLabs](https://elevenlabs.io).

1. Sign in.
2. Go to your profile/account settings.
3. Find `API Keys`.
4. Create/copy an API key.

That becomes:

```bash
ELEVENLABS_API_KEY=[REDACTED:elevenlabs-api-key]
```

Now choose Cortex’s voice:

1. Go to `Voices`.
2. Pick a voice from your library or voice library.
3. Open the voice details.
4. Copy the `Voice ID`.

That becomes:

```bash
ELEVENLABS_VOICE_ID=your_voice_id_here
```

Keep this as-is:

```bash
CORTEX_TTS_PROVIDER=elevenlabs
ELEVENLABS_TTS_MODEL=eleven_turbo_v2_5
```

**3. Groq**
Go to [Groq Console](https://console.groq.com).

1. Sign in.
2. Go to `API Keys`.
3. Create a new key.
4. Copy it.

That becomes:

```bash
GROQ_API_KEY=[REDACTED:groq-api-key]
```

Keep this as-is for fast Sabi-style speech-to-text:

```bash
CORTEX_STT_PROVIDER=groq
CORTEX_STT_MODEL=whisper-large-v3-turbo
```

**4. Add Everything To Vercel**
Go to [Vercel Dashboard](https://vercel.com/dashboard).

1. Open the existing Cortex project.
2. Go to `Settings`.
3. Go to `Environment Variables`.
4. Add each variable below.
5. Select `Production`.
6. Also select `Preview` if you want preview deployments to work too.
7. Save.

Paste these names exactly:

```bash
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_DSN=
SENTRY_DSN=
SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN=
[REDACTED:sentry-auth-token]
SENTRY_PROJECT=
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT=production
SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT=production
NEXT_PUBLIC_SENTRY_TRACES_SAMPLE_RATE=0.05
SENTRY_TRACES_SAMPLE_RATE=0.05

CORTEX_TTS_PROVIDER=elevenlabs
ELEVENLABS_API_KEY=
[REDACTED:elevenlabs-api-key]
ELEVENLABS_TTS_MODEL=eleven_turbo_v2_5

CORTEX_STT_PROVIDER=groq
GROQ_API_KEY=
[REDACTED:groq-api-key]
```

**5. Redeploy**
In Vercel:

1. Go to `Deployments`.
2. Click the latest production deployment.
3. Click `Redeploy`.
4. Make sure it uses the latest code from Git once we push/commit this.

**6. Check If Voice Is Ready**
After deploy, open:

```bash
https://cortex.eduforequality.org/api/voice/health
```

You want to see:

```json
"status": "ready"
```

If it says `missing_configuration`, it will tell you whether it is missing `ELEVENLABS_API_KEY` or `GROQ_API_KEY`.

Tiny but important: do not paste the API keys into chat. Put them directly into Vercel.

## Codex · 2026-06-30T07:04:39.615Z · final_answer

Done. `/voice` is now much closer to Sabi.

Open: [https://cortex.eduforequality.org/voice](https://cortex.eduforequality.org/voice)

What changed:

- No more click-to-record for every turn.
- Click `Start session` once.
- Allow mic access.
- Then just talk.
- Cortex auto-detects when you start speaking.
- Cortex stops capture after silence.
- It sends audio to Groq STT.
- It sends the transcript to Cortex chat.
- It speaks back with ElevenLabs.
- Then it automatically returns to listening.

Browser reality: Safari/Chrome still require one initial user gesture for microphone permission, so `Start session` has to exist. But after that, you should not need to click for every sentence.

If the page shows 404, you’re signed out in that browser. Sign in first at [https://cortex.eduforequality.org](https://cortex.eduforequality.org), then open `/voice`.

I left the other dirty files alone; the hands-free voice change is pushed in commit `2fee711`.

## Codex · 2026-07-01T00:55:39.479Z · final_answer

Done. I took over the stranded backfill batch and shipped it.

I committed and pushed `8f58e50`:

`Add bounded Gmail and Calendar history backfill`

It is deployed on Vercel as `dpl_FVhST1GaTC86o2Vs9PWaA7LBp1C4`, aliased to:

[https://cortex.eduforequality.org](https://cortex.eduforequality.org)

Verification passed:

- `git diff --check`
- `npx tsc --noEmit`
- `npm run redteam:guardrails`
- `npm run build`
- post-deploy `npm run check:launch`: `28 ok`, `0 fail`, only expected skipped slow LoRA smoke warning
- production readiness: `ok`

What changed:

- Calendar now syncs all accessible calendars, not just primary.
- Calendar supports `Sync latest` and `Backfill history`.
- Daily cron now advances Gmail history and Calendar history in bounded chunks.
- Cortex will keep deepening your context automatically over time.

Right now, you do this:

1. Open [https://cortex.eduforequality.org](https://cortex.eduforequality.org) and sign in.
2. Go to `Dashboard`; check the signed-in launch smoke/readiness panel.
3. Go to `Integrations`.
4. For Gmail accounts, click `Backfill history`.
5. For Calendar, click `Sync latest`, then `Backfill history`.
6. Go to `Settings` → `Personal Model` → click `Test Serving`.
7. Go to `Talk to Cortex` and test voice.

Do **not** touch RunPod right now. Personal model adapter v5 is already deployed and has handled real inference. The main remaining owner-side gap is Meta/Instagram/WhatsApp setup, because live DB still has `0` Instagram/WhatsApp docs until the official Meta setup is completed.
